. Moreover, it is also true that
\cos(\theta)=\frac{x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho}\geq\frac{L\varepsilon}{2\rho}>\frac{1}{2}
. Hence, in this case the derivative of
G
is not singular and we can estimate
\left|\nabla_{x}^{n}G(x)\right|\leq\frac{C_{G,n}2^{n-{\frac{1}{2}}}}{\rho^{{\frac{1}{2}}+n}}
. Moreover, if
\eta\in B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)
, then from one hand we have
\frac{3}{4}\rho(x)<\rho(\eta)<\frac{5}{4}\rho(x)
and on the other hand
\cos(\theta(\eta))\rho(\eta)=\eta_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon>\frac{L}{4}\varepsilon
, thus
\cos\left(\theta(\eta)\right)>\frac{1}{5}

\sup_{\eta\in B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)}\left[\frac{1}{\cos^{\frac{5}{2}}\left(\theta(\eta)\right)\rho^{\frac{7}{2}}(x)}\right]\leq 5^{\frac{5}{2}}\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)^{\frac{7}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\rho(x)}\right)^{\frac{7}{2}}\leq\frac{2C}{\varepsilon L}\left(\frac{1}{\rho(x)}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}},

where at the end we used that
\rho(x)>\frac{L\varepsilon}{2}
for all
x\in\Omega
. For the computation of the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}
for the function
W
we will use the Taylor expansion of this function on the ball
B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)
. The error terms as defined in (
4.68
), (
4.69
) and (
4.70
) satisfy then

\begin{split}\left|E_{F}^{4}(\eta,x)\right|\leq\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)^{4}&\frac{C_{F,4}}{\rho(x)^{4}}|x-\eta|^{4},\;\;\;\left|E_{H}^{4}(\eta,x)\right|\leq b\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)^{6}\frac{C_{H,4}}{\rho(x)^{6}}|x-\eta|^{4}\varepsilon^{2}\;\text{ and }\\
&\left|E_{G}^{3}(\eta,x)\right|\leq a\frac{C_{G,3}}{L\rho(x)^{\frac{5}{2}}}\frac{|x-\eta|^{3}}{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

(4.72)

We can now proceed with the estimate for the operator. Applying the Taylor expansion we obtain

\begin{split}&\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(W)(x)=W(x)-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)W(\eta)\;d\eta-\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)W(\eta)\;d\eta\\
\geq&W(x)-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)W(\eta)\;d\eta-\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)W(\eta)\;d\eta\\
=&W(x)\left(1-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)\;d\eta\right)-\left(\nabla_{x}W(x)\right)\cdot\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)(\eta-x)\;d\eta\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)(\eta-x)^{\top}\nabla^{2}_{x}W(x)(\eta-x)\;d\eta\\
&-\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{D^{\alpha}(F+H)(x)}{\alpha!}\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)(\eta-x)^{\alpha}\;d\eta\\
&-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)(E_{F}^{4}+E_{H}^{4}+E_{G}^{3})(\eta,x)\;d\eta-\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)W(\eta)\;d\eta\end{split}

(4.73)

For the terms of the Taylor expansion containing the first and third derivatives we use now the symmetry of the integral in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, while for the second degree derivative terms we use the Laplacian identity as in equation (
4.71
), together with the fact that
F
is harmonic while
H
and
G
are super-harmonic as in equations (
4.66
) and (
4.67
). Denoting by
C_{F},C_{G},C_{H}
constants depending on
F,G
resp.
H
only and changing the coordinates
y\mapsto(\eta-x)
we estimate using the estimates for the error terms as in (
4.72
)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(W)(x)\geq-C_{F}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{2}|y|}\;dy+\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{\rho^{3}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}|y|}{4\pi\varepsilon^{4}}\;dy\right)\\
&-C_{H}b\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{\rho^{3}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{2}|y|}\;dy+\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{3}}\;dy+\frac{\varepsilon^{5}}{\rho^{5}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}|y|}{4\pi\varepsilon^{4}}\;dy\right)\\
&-C_{G}a\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{2}|y|}\;dy+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{L^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho^{2}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{3}}\;dy\right)\\
&+\frac{2b}{3}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}}-C_{F}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}}-C_{H}b\frac{\varepsilon^{6}}{\rho^{6}}+\frac{1}{12}\frac{a\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{2}}}{\cos^{\frac{3}{2}}(\theta)\rho^{\frac{5}{2}}}-a\frac{C_{G}\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{2}}}{L\rho(x)^{\frac{5}{2}}}-(\pi+a)e^{-\frac{\rho}{4\varepsilon}}\\
\geq&\frac{2}{3}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}}\left(b-\frac{3}{2}(C_{F}+\pi\tilde{C})-\frac{3C_{H}b}{2L^{2}}-\frac{3C_{F}\tilde{C}}{2L}-\frac{3bC_{H}\tilde{C}}{2L^{3}}\right)+\frac{a\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{2}}}{\rho^{\frac{5}{2}}}\left(\frac{1}{12}-\frac{C_{G}}{L}-\frac{\tilde{C}}{L^{\frac{3}{2}}}-\frac{C_{G}\tilde{C}}{L^{3}}\right).\end{split}

(4.74)

Moreover, we used that
\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon}>\frac{L}{2}>1
and the well-known estimates
\int_{\frac{r}{\varepsilon}}^{\infty}e^{-x}x^{n}\leq C_{n}e^{-\frac{r}{2\varepsilon}}\leq\tilde{C}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{r^{4}}
for
n=0,1,2,3
, as well as
\frac{\varepsilon^{n}}{\rho^{n}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho}<\frac{2}{L}
and the fact that
0<\cos(\theta)<1
. Choosing

b>3C_{F}(1+2\tilde{C}\pi)\;\;\;\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\;\;\;L>\max\{\tilde{C},\;\sqrt{6C_{H}},\;(24C_{G}+12)^{2}\}.

(4.75)

We can conclude

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(W)(x)\geq 0.

(4.76)

Case 2:
\rho(x)>L\varepsilon
and
d(x)<\varepsilon

We consider now the case when
\rho(x)>L\varepsilon
with
d(x)<\varepsilon
for
|x_{2}|,|x_{3}|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+3\delta}
and
0<x_{1}<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+3\delta}
. First of all, we see that if
d(x)<\varepsilon
then also
x_{1}<2\varepsilon
. This is true since for all these
x
the distance can be estimated
\varepsilon>d(x)=|x-z|
for a unique
\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)=z\in\partial\Omega
. Hence,
x_{1}<\varepsilon+z_{1}
since also by the convexity and for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small we have
z_{1}\leq x_{1}
. Thus, if
z_{1}\geq\varepsilon
approximating the curvature by a sphere of radius
R
from the interior tangent to
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
we see that
z_{i}\geq C(R)\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
for an
i\in\{2,3\}
and hence
d(x)=|x-z|\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}(C(R)-\varepsilon^{3\delta})>\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. This implies a contradiction, and thus
x_{1}<2\varepsilon
. Let us consider now
\eta\in B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega
, then
\frac{3}{4}\rho(x)<\rho(\eta)<\frac{5}{4}\rho(x)
. Hence, using the definition of cosine

\begin{split}\cos\left(\theta(x)\right)-\cos&\left(\theta(\eta)\right)=\frac{x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho(x)}-\frac{\eta_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho(\eta)}\leq\frac{x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho(x)}-\frac{\frac{2L}{5}\varepsilon}{\rho(x)}\\
=&\frac{1}{\rho(x)}\left(x_{1}+\frac{1}{10}L\varepsilon\right)=\frac{1}{\rho(x)}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{2}+\frac{L}{4}\varepsilon\right)+\frac{1}{\rho(x)}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{2}-\frac{3L}{20}\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{\cos\left(\theta(x)\right)}{2}\end{split}

if
L>\frac{20}{3}
. This implies that
\cos\left(\theta(\eta)\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\cos\left(\theta(x)\right)
and therefore using
\cos(\theta)\rho=x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon\geq\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon
we obtain

\sup_{\eta\in B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}\left[\cos^{-\frac{5}{2}}\left(\theta(\eta)\right)\rho^{-\frac{7}{2}}(x)\right]\leq 5^{\frac{5}{2}}\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)^{\frac{7}{2}}\cos\left(\theta(x)\right)^{-\frac{5}{2}}\rho(x)^{-\frac{7}{2}}\leq\frac{2C}{\varepsilon L}\cos\left(\theta(x)\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\rho(x)^{-\frac{5}{2}}.

(4.77)

We can now proceed similarly as we did in equations (
4.73
) and (
4.74
). We apply the Taylor expansion on the set
B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega
where
W
is smooth.

\begin{split}&\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(W)(x)=W(x)-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)W(\eta)\;d\eta-\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)W(\eta)\;d\eta\\
\geq&W(x)-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)W(\eta)\;d\eta-\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)(F+G)(\eta)\;d\eta\\
=&W(x)\left(1-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)\;d\eta\right)-\left(\nabla_{x}W(x)\right)\cdot\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)(\eta-x)\;d\eta\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)(\eta-x)^{\top}\nabla^{2}_{x}W(x)(\eta-x)\;d\eta\\
&-\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{D^{\alpha}(F+H)(x)}{\alpha!}\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)(\eta-x)^{\alpha}\;d\eta\\
&-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}\frac{e^{-\frac{\left|{\eta-x}\right|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon\left|{\eta-x}\right|^{2}}(E_{F}^{4}+E_{H}^{4}+E_{G}^{3})(\eta,x)\;d\eta-\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)(F+G)(\eta)\;d\eta\\
\end{split}

(4.78)

Once again we use the symmetry for the first and third order term on the set
B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)\subset B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega
estimating the integral on
B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\left(\Omega\setminus B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)\right)
by the integral on the larger set
B^{c}_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)
. Once more we need the identity for the Laplacian on the set
B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)
too. We estimate the error terms of
F
and
H
by (
4.72
) and the error term of
G
by the last equation (
4.77
). Hence, we estimate

\begin{split}\geq&-C_{F}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{2}|y|}\;dy+\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{\rho^{3}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}|y|}{4\pi\varepsilon^{4}}\;dy\right)\\
&-C_{H}b\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{\rho^{3}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{2}|y|}\;dy+\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{3}}\;dy+\frac{\varepsilon^{5}}{\rho^{5}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}|y|}{4\pi\varepsilon^{4}}\;dy\right)\\
&-C_{G}a\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{2}|y|}\;dy+\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(0)}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{3}}\;dy\right)\\
&+\frac{2b}{3}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}}-C_{F}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}}-C_{H}b\frac{\varepsilon^{6}}{\rho^{6}}+\frac{1}{12}\frac{a\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{2}}}{\cos^{\frac{3}{2}}(\theta)\rho^{\frac{5}{2}}}-a\frac{C_{G}\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{2}}}{L\cos^{\frac{3}{2}}(\theta)\rho(x)^{\frac{5}{2}}}-(\pi+a)e^{-\frac{\rho}{4\varepsilon}}\\
\geq&-Ce^{-\frac{d}{8\varepsilon}}\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho}\left(a+C_{F}+\frac{aC_{G}}{L^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\frac{bC_{H}}{L^{2}}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}}\left(b-\frac{3}{2}C_{F}-\frac{3C_{H}b}{2L^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{12}\frac{a\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{2}}}{\cos^{\frac{3}{2}}(\theta)\rho^{\frac{5}{2}}}\left(1-12\frac{C_{G}}{L}\right).\end{split}

(4.79)

In all the estimate above we used that
e^{-\frac{\rho}{4\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{C\varepsilon}{\rho}e^{-\frac{d}{8\varepsilon}}
, since
\rho>L\varepsilon>d(x)
. Hence, choosing

b>3C_{F}\;\;\;\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\;\;\;L>\max\left\{\frac{20}{3},\;12C_{G},\;\sqrt{3C_{H}}\right\}

(4.80)

we conclude using
L\geq\sqrt{L}

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(W)(x)\geq-\frac{C(a,\pi,b,C_{F})}{\sqrt{L}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}.

(4.81)

Case 3:
\rho(x)>L\varepsilon
and
d(x)>\varepsilon

We can finish the proof of this Lemma by estimating the operator acting on
W
when
\rho(x)>L\varepsilon
and
d(x)>\varepsilon
for
x\in\mathcal{C}_{3\delta}
. We notice that by definition
d(x)\leq\rho(x)
. In this case, we estimate first the operator acting only on
F+H
proceeding as for the derivation of (
4.78
) and (
4.79
) with the only difference that this time

\begin{split}(F+H)(x)\left(1-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)\;d\eta\right)\geq&-\frac{b\varepsilon^{2}\cos(\theta)^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{d}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)\;d\eta\\
\geq&-\frac{b\varepsilon^{2}}{\rho^{2}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{4\varepsilon}}\geq-\frac{b}{L^{2}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{4\varepsilon}}.\end{split}

Hence we get for
F+H

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(F+H)(x)\geq&-Ce^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{L}\left(C_{F}+\pi+\frac{bC_{H}}{L^{2}}+\frac{b}{L}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}}\left(b-\frac{3}{2}C_{F}-\frac{3C_{H}b}{2L^{2}}\right)\\
\geq&-Ce^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{L}(\pi+2b),\end{split}

for
b
and
L
as in (
4.80
). We consider now the operator acting only on
G
and we compute

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(G)(x)&=G(x)-\int_{B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)G(\eta)\;d\eta-\int_{B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega\setminus B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)G(\eta)\;d\eta\\
&-\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)G(\eta)\;d\eta\\
\geq&G(x)-\int_{B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)G(\eta)\;d\eta-\frac{2a}{\sqrt{3L}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{4\varepsilon}}-\frac{Ca}{L}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}.\end{split}

We used
\rho(\eta)\geq\frac{3}{4}\rho(x)\geq\frac{3}{4}L\varepsilon
for
\eta\in B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega\setminus B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)
, the integral on
B_{\frac{\rho(x)}{4}}(x)\cap\Omega\setminus B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)
can be estimated from above by the one on
B^{c}_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)
and the last integral was estimated as in (
4.78
) and (
4.79
) using
e^{-\frac{\rho(x)}{4\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{C}{L}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}
. In order to estimate the integral on
B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)
we will expand
G
by Taylor and therefore we have to control the singularity
\left(\cos(\theta)\right)^{-\frac{5}{2}}
of the error term
E^{3}_{G}
defined in (
4.70
). Let hence
\eta\in B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)
for
d(x)>\varepsilon
. Since
d(x)\leq x_{1}
, we know that
x_{1}>\varepsilon
and also that
\eta_{1}>x_{1}-\frac{d(x)}{4}>\frac{3}{4}x_{1}
. That
d(x)\leq x_{1}
can be proved in the following way. Let
z=\left\{x-te_{1}:t\geq 0\right\}\cap\partial\Omega
, hence
z_{1}\geq 0
. Then
d(x)\leq|x-z|=x_{1}-z_{1}\leq x_{1}
. We can thus estimate

\begin{split}\cos\left(\theta(\eta)\right)=&\frac{\eta_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho(\eta)}>\frac{x_{1}-\frac{d(x)}{4}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho(\eta)}>\frac{\frac{3}{4}x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho(\eta)}\\
=&\frac{3}{4}\frac{x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho(\eta)}+\frac{1}{8}\frac{L\varepsilon}{\rho(\eta)}>\frac{3}{4}\frac{x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho(\eta)}>\frac{3}{5}\frac{x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon}{\rho(x)}=\frac{3}{5}\cos\left(\theta(x)\right),\end{split}

where we used that
\rho(\eta)<\rho(x)+\frac{d(x)}{4}<\frac{5}{4}\rho(x)
since
\rho(x)\geq x_{1}+\frac{L}{2\varepsilon}\geq d(x)+\frac{L}{2\varepsilon}>d(x)
. Similarly
\rho(\eta)>\frac{3}{4}\rho(x)
. Hence,

\sup_{\eta\in B_{\frac{d(x)}{4}}(x)}\left[\cos^{-\frac{5}{2}}\left(\theta(\eta)\right)\rho^{-\frac{7}{2}}(x)\right]\leq\left(\frac{5}{3}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)^{\frac{7}{2}}\cos\left(\theta(x)\right)^{-\frac{5}{2}}\rho(x)^{-\frac{7}{2}}\leq\frac{2C}{\varepsilon L}\cos\left(\theta(x)\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\rho(x)^{-\frac{5}{2}},

where we used in addition
\cos(\theta)\rho=x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon\geq\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon
.
Now we are ready to conclude the estimate for
G
. We proceed as we did in (
4.73
) and in (
4.78
) using the Taylor expansion. We have then for
L
as in (
4.80
)

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(G)(x)&\geq-\frac{2a}{\sqrt{3L}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{4\varepsilon}}-\frac{Ca}{L}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}-\frac{aC_{G}}{L^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{12}\frac{a\varepsilon^{\frac{5}{2}}}{\cos^{\frac{3}{2}}(\theta)\rho^{\frac{5}{2}}}\left(1-12\frac{C_{G}}{L}\right)\\
\geq&-\frac{C(a)}{\sqrt{L}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}},\end{split}

Thus, we obtain once more

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(W)(x)\geq-\frac{C(a,\pi,C_{F},b)}{\sqrt{L}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}.

(4.82)

Equations (
4.76
), (
4.81
) and (
4.82
) imply the last claim (
4.65
). Indeed, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon,L,\delta
such that for
L>L_{0}
as in (
4.75
) and (
4.80
) and for
0<\varepsilon<1
sufficiently small such that
L<\varepsilon^{-\frac{1-8\delta}{2}}
,
x\in\mathcal{C}_{3\delta}
it holds

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(W)(x)\geq-\frac{C}{\sqrt{L}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}.

This conclude the proof of the Lemma.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
4.2
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

Let
W_{\varepsilon,L}
be as in (
4.57
). Lemma
4.4
implies for
\overline{W}:=\sum_{i=2}^{3}\left(W^{+}_{i}(x)+W^{-}_{i}(x)\right)
that for any
i=1,2,3

\begin{cases}\overline{W}(x)\geq 0&\text{ if }x\in\Omega;\\
\overline{W}(x)\geq\frac{\pi}{2}-\arctan(2)&\text{ if }|x_{i}|\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+3\delta};\\
\overline{W}(x)\leq C\varepsilon^{\alpha}&\text{ if }|x_{i}|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta};\\
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}(\overline{W})(x)\geq-\frac{C}{\sqrt{L}}e^{-\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}&\text{ if }|x_{i}|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+3\delta}.\end{cases}

(4.83)

Moreover, Theorem
4.2
imply that there exists a constant
\tilde{C}(\Omega,g_{\nu})
such that
\frac{\tilde{C}}{\sqrt{L}}\phi_{\frac{1}{8},\varepsilon}
satisfies

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\tilde{C}}{\sqrt{L}}\phi_{\frac{1}{8},\varepsilon}\right)(x)\geq\frac{C}{\sqrt{L}}e^{-{\frac{d(x)}{8\varepsilon}}}\;\;\;\;\;\forall x\in\Omega,

where
C
is the constant in (
4.83
). Theorem
4.2
implies also
0\leq\phi_{A,\varepsilon}(x)\leq C(\Omega)<\infty
for any
0<A<1
and any
0<A\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. Hence,
W_{\varepsilon,L}
is non negative and we can conclude equation (
4.52
) for
\alpha=\frac{\delta}{2}
, for some constant
C(\Omega,g_{\nu})>0
independent of
\delta,p\in\partial\Omega,\varepsilon,L
and for
L>0
and
0<\varepsilon<1
sufficiently small such that
L>\varepsilon^{-\frac{2}{1-8\delta}}
.
∎

The properties of
W_{\varepsilon,L}
imply now the boundedness of
|u^{\varepsilon}-\overline{u}|
near the boundary
\partial\Omega
.

Corollary 4.1
.

Let
0<\delta<\frac{1}{16}
. There exists a constant
C>0
, a large
L>0
and an
\alpha>0
independent of
x,p,\varepsilon
such that

\left|\overline{u}\left(\frac{R_{p}(\cdot)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},p\right)-u^{\varepsilon}\right|(x)\leq C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)

for all
|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
.

Proof.

Let
L>0
large as in Proposition
4.2
and let
0<\varepsilon<1
sufficiently small such that
L<\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\beta}}
for
\beta=\frac{1-8\delta}{2}
. Let
\delta<\frac{1}{16}
and
\alpha=\frac{\delta}{2}
. If
|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
with
x\in\Omega
then
0<x\cdot(-N_{p})<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
and
|\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{i}|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
for
i=2,3
. Let us consider
W_{\varepsilon,L}
as defined in (
4.57
). As we know,
\overline{u}\left(\frac{R_{p}(\cdot)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},p\right)
and
u^{\varepsilon}
are both uniformly bounded, independently of
p
. Let us call
K>0
this bound. Moreover,
W_{\varepsilon,L}\geq\frac{\pi}{2}-\arctan(2):=\tilde{C}>0
. Hence, for all
|\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{i}|\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+3\delta}
we have that
\frac{K}{\tilde{C}}W_{\varepsilon,L}(x)-\left|\overline{u}\left(\frac{R_{p}(\cdot)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},p\right)-u^{\varepsilon}\right|(x)\geq 0
. The function
\frac{K}{\tilde{C}}W_{\varepsilon,L}
is also continuous and satisfies
\frac{K}{\tilde{C}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(W_{\varepsilon,L}\right)\geq C\varepsilon^{\delta}\frac{K}{\tilde{C}}e^{-\frac{Ad(x)}{\varepsilon}}
for all
|\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{i}|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+3\delta}
. Using the maximum principle of Theorem
4.1
and the estimate for the operator acting on
\overline{u}\left(\frac{R_{p}(\cdot)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},p\right)-u^{\varepsilon}
in Lemma
4.3
we obtain

\left|\overline{u}\left(\frac{R_{p}(\cdot)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},p\right)-u^{\varepsilon}\right|(x)\leq C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)

for all
|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
.
∎

This is a key estimate for the proof of the convergence of the exact solutions
u^{\varepsilon}
up to the boundary
\partial\Omega
.

4.4
Convergence of
u^{\varepsilon}
to the solution of the new boundary value problem

This last section is devoted to the proof of the pointwise convergence of
u^{\varepsilon}
to the solution of the Laplace equation in
\Omega
with boundary value
\overline{u}_{\infty}
(cf. Proposition
3.1
).
We proceed defining new regions. We define
\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{x\in\Omega:d(x)>\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}\right\}
,
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{x\in\Omega:\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+6\delta}<d(x)\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}\right\}
and their union
\Omega_{\varepsilon}=\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\cup\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. We also define for
0<\sigma\ll 1
independent of
\varepsilon
the set
\Omega^{\sigma}:=\Omega\cup\left\{x\in\Omega^{c}:d(x)<\sigma\right\}
. We recall the continuous projection
\pi_{\partial\Omega}
as given in (
1.13
).

Figure 9
:
Decomposition of
\Omega

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and
0<\delta<1\frac{1}{16}
. Let
C,L,\alpha
be as in the Corollary
4.1
. Then it holds

\sup_{x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\left|\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+\tilde{\omega}_{1}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-6\delta}\right),

(4.84)

where
\tilde{\omega}_{1}(r)=Ce^{-\frac{r}{2}}
for a suitable constant
C>0
.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.2
there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
p\in\partial\Omega
such that
\left|\overline{u}(y,p)-\overline{u}_{\infty}(p)\right|\leq Ce^{-\frac{y}{2}}=\tilde{\omega}_{1}(y)
. Hence, let
x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. Then

\begin{split}&\left|\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\\
\leq&\left|u^{\varepsilon}(x)-\overline{u}\left(\frac{R_{\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)}(x)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)\right|-\left|\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)-\overline{u}\left(\frac{R_{\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)}(x)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)\right|\\
<&C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+\tilde{\omega}_{1}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-6\delta}\right),\end{split}

where we used the result of Corollary
4.1
and the fact that since
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+6\delta}<d(x)<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
then we have that
\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}+6\delta}\leq\left|\frac{R_{\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)}(x)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right|\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
. Moreover, since
\delta<\frac{1}{16}
, we have that
\frac{1}{2}-6\delta>\frac{1}{8}>¨0
.
∎

We recall first the definition of
v
as solution to the Laplacian (cf. (
4.1
))

\begin{cases}-\Delta v(x)=0&x\in\Omega,\\
v(p)=\overline{u}_{\infty}(p)&p\in\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Clearly by the uniformly continuity of
\overline{u}_{\infty}
we have that
v\in C^{\infty}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
. We call
\omega_{2}
its modulus of continuity. Further, we need to consider another function, harmonic on the larger domain
\Omega^{\sigma}

\begin{cases}-\Delta v_{\sigma}(x)=0&x\in\Omega^{\sigma},\\
v(x)=\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)&x\in\partial\Omega^{\sigma}.\end{cases}

We recall that
\pi_{\partial\Omega}:\partial\Omega^{\sigma}\to\partial\Omega
is a continuous bijection if
\sigma>0
small enough and therefore
v_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}\left(\Omega^{\sigma}\right)\cap C\left(\overline{\Omega^{\sigma}}\right)
. Denoting
\omega
its modulus of continuity a simple application of the maximum principle for harmonic functions implies
\sup_{x\in\Omega}\left|v_{\sigma}(x)-v(x)\right|\leq\omega(\sigma)
. Indeed,
v_{\sigma}-v
is harmonic on
\Omega
and thus the maximum must be attained on
\partial\Omega
. Hence, using that
\pi_{\partial\Omega}
is a bijection we obtain

v_{\sigma}(x)-v(x)\leq\max\limits_{x\in\partial\Omega}\left(v_{\sigma}(x)-v(x)\right)=\max\limits_{x\in\partial\Omega^{\sigma}}\left(v_{\sigma}(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x))-v_{\sigma}(x)\right)\leq\omega(\sigma),

(4.85)

since
|x-\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)|=\sigma
for
x\in\partial\Omega^{\sigma}
. The same can be estimated for
v-v_{\sigma}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
x\in\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
. Then

\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(v_{\sigma}-u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\right|\leq C(\Omega,g_{\nu})e^{-\frac{Ad(x)}{\varepsilon}}\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}\right)+\frac{C}{\sigma^{3}}\varepsilon^{3},

(4.86)

for some constant
C(\Omega,g_{\nu})>0
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.

Proof.

We already know that in general we always have the estimate

|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)|\leq Ce^{-\frac{d}{\varepsilon}}
(cf. Theorem
4.2
). Since for
x\in\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
the distance to the boundary satisfies
d(x)>\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
, we estimate for these points

|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)|\leq Ce^{-\frac{d}{\varepsilon}}\leq Ce^{-\frac{d}{2\varepsilon}}2\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}}\leq Ce^{-\frac{d}{2\varepsilon}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-4\delta}=Ce^{-\frac{d}{2\varepsilon}}\varepsilon^{\beta},

(4.87)

where
\beta=\frac{1}{2}-4\delta>0
for
\delta<\frac{1}{16}
.

Let us consider now the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}
acting on
v_{\sigma}
. We apply as usual the Taylor expansion to
v_{\sigma}(\eta)=v_{\sigma}(x)+\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\cdot(\eta-x)+\frac{1}{2}(\eta-x)^{\top}\nabla_{x}^{2}v_{\sigma}(x)(\eta-x)+E^{3}(\eta,x)
. Since
x\in B_{\sigma}(x)\subset\Omega^{\sigma}
for all
x\in\Omega
by the harmonicity of
v_{\sigma}
we obtain

\left|\partial^{\alpha}v_{\sigma}(x)\right|\leq C(|\alpha|)\frac{\Arrowvert v_{\sigma}\Arrowvert_{\infty}\sigma^{3}}{\sigma^{3+|\alpha|}}=C(|\alpha|)\frac{\Arrowvert\overline{u}_{\infty}\Arrowvert_{\infty}}{\sigma^{|\alpha|}}\leq\frac{C}{\sigma^{|\alpha|}},

where we used that
v_{\sigma}
attains its maximum on the boundary and that
\overline{u}_{\infty}
is uniformly bounded. Hence, we calculate for
x\in\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small

\begin{split}\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(v_{\sigma}\right)(x)\right|=&\left|v_{\sigma}(x)-\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)\left[v_{\sigma}(x)+\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\cdot(\eta-x)+\frac{1}{2}(\eta-x)^{\top}\nabla_{x}^{2}v_{\sigma}(x)(\eta-x)\right]\;d\eta\right|\\
&+\left|\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)E^{3}(\eta,x)\;d\eta\right|\\
\leq&v_{\sigma}(x)\int_{B^{c}_{d(x)(x)}}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)\;d\eta+\frac{C\varepsilon}{\sigma}\int_{B^{c}_{d(x)(0)}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{2}|y|}\;dy+\frac{C\varepsilon^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\int_{B^{c}_{d(x)(0)}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{3}}\;dy\\
&+\frac{C\varepsilon^{3}}{\sigma^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{3}}\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}\;dy\\
\leq&Ce^{-\frac{d(x)}{\varepsilon}}\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}\right)+\frac{C\varepsilon^{3}}{\sigma^{3}},\end{split}

(4.88)

where we used the symmetry of the kernel for the first order term, the fact that
v_{\sigma}
is harmonic for the second order term, the boundedness of
\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-r}r^{3}\;dr
and the fact that
\Omega^{c}\subset B^{c}_{d(x)}(x)
. Equations (
4.87
) and (
4.88
) yield (
4.86
).
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon>0
small enough. Then the following uniform bound holds

\left|v_{\sigma}(x)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq\omega(\sigma)+\omega_{2}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}\right)+C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+\tilde{\omega}_{1}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-6\delta}\right).

Proof.

Let
x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. Then,
d(x)=\left|x-\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
. Hence,
\left|v(x)-\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)\right|<\omega_{2}(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta})
. Thus using equation (
4.85
) and Lemma
4.5
we conclude

\begin{split}\left|v_{\sigma}(x)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq&\left|v_{\sigma}(x)-v(x)\right|+\left|v(x)-\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)\right|+\left|\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\\
\leq&\omega(\sigma)+\omega_{2}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}\right)+C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+\tilde{\omega}_{1}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-6\delta}\right).\end{split}

(4.89)

∎

With an application of the maximum principle on
\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\cup\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
we can prove the convergence of
u^{\varepsilon}
to the harmonic function
v

\varepsilon\to 0
.

Theorem 4.3
.

u^{\varepsilon}
converges to
v
uniformly in every compact set.

Proof.

As we mentioned above we will use the maximum principle for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}
. We start estimating for
x\in\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
this operator acting on
v_{\sigma}-u^{\varepsilon}
. Thus, for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small

\begin{split}\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}\left(v_{\sigma}-u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\right|\leq&\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(v_{\sigma}-u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\right|+\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\varepsilon}}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)\left(v_{\sigma}(\eta)-u^{\varepsilon}(\eta)\right)\;d\eta\\
\leq Ce^{-\frac{d(x)}{\varepsilon}}&\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}\right)+\frac{C\varepsilon^{3}}{\sigma^{3}}+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{8\delta-1}{2}}}{2}\right)\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{|\eta-x|^{2}}\;d\eta\\
\leq Ce^{-\frac{d(x)}{2\varepsilon}}&\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1-8\delta}{2}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}\right)+\frac{C\varepsilon^{3}}{\sigma^{3}}+C\left(\Omega\right)\varepsilon^{3},\end{split}

(4.90)

where we used Lemma
4.6
, the fact that if
\eta\in\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\varepsilon}
and
0<\varepsilon<2^{-\frac{1}{2\delta}}
we have
|x-\eta|>\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}(1-\varepsilon^{2\delta})>\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}}{2}
and that
d(x)\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}
. Moreover, we used that
\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega
and that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C_{n}
such that
|x|^{n}e^{-|x|}\leq C_{n}
. We chose here
\delta<\frac{1}{72}
and
n=9
.

We now set for
C>0
the maximum between the constants appearing in estimates (
4.89
) and (
4.90
)

K_{\varepsilon}=C\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1-8\delta}{2}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma^{3}}+\omega(\sigma)+\omega_{2}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+4\delta}\right)+C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+\tilde{\omega}_{1}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-6\delta}\right)\right).

Analogously to Theorem
4.2
we define

\psi(x)=K_{\varepsilon}2C_{3}\left(\left(C_{1}-\left|x\right|^{2}\right)+C_{2}\left[\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{1+\left(\frac{d_{\varepsilon}(x)}{2\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\right)\wedge\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{1+\left(\frac{\mu R}{4\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\right)\right]\right),

where
C_{1}(\Omega):=2\max_{x\in\overline{\Omega}}\left|x\right|^{2}+2\;\textnormal{diam}\left(\Omega\right)^{2}+4\;\textnormal{diam}\left(\Omega\right)+4
and
C_{2}(\Omega),\>C_{3}(\Omega)
are independent of
\varepsilon
. We denote
d_{\varepsilon}:=\text{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{\varepsilon})
. We claim that
\psi
is a supersolution for
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}
. This is true because the geometrical properties of
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, in particular its regularity and the estimate for the radii of curvature, are identical to those for
\Omega
. More precisely, if
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
sufficiently small the minimal radius of curvature
R_{\varepsilon}
for
\partial\Omega_{\varepsilon}
satisfies
\frac{R}{2}<R_{\varepsilon}<R
. Moreover,
d_{\varepsilon}(x)\leq d(x)
and therefore
\Arrowvert\nabla_{x}^{2}d_{\varepsilon}\Arrowvert_{op}\leq\frac{1}{(1-\mu)\frac{R}{2}}
for
\mu\in(0,1)
and
d_{\varepsilon}(x)<\frac{R}{2}\mu
. Hence,
C_{2},C_{3},\gamma,\mu
can be chosen as in Theorem
4.2
. Thus,
\psi
has uniform upper and lower bound independently on
\varepsilon
and all calculations we performed in Lemma
4.1
and Lemma
4.2
apply also for
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\psi)(x)
.
Hence, since
d_{\varepsilon}(x)\leq d(x)
we estimate using equation (
4.35
)

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}\left(\psi\right)(x)\geq K_{\varepsilon}\left(e^{-\frac{d_{\varepsilon}(x)}{2\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{2}\right)\geq K_{\varepsilon}\left(e^{-\frac{d(x)}{2\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{2}\right).

(4.91)

We remark that
\psi\geq 8K_{\varepsilon}C_{3}
by the definition of
C_{1}
. Hence, multiplying
\psi
by
K=\max\{1,\frac{1}{8C_{3}}\}
we have
K\psi\geq K_{\varepsilon}
. We apply now use the maximum principle for
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}
acting on
K\psi-(v_{\sigma}-u^{\varepsilon})
. Indeed,
K\psi(x)\geq|v_{\sigma}(x)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)|
if
x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
by Lemma
4.7
and the definition of
K_{\varepsilon}
. We notice also that estimates (
4.90
) and (
4.91
) imply
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}\left(K\psi-\left(v_{\sigma}(x)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right)\right)(x)\geq 0
as well as
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}\left(K\psi-\left(u^{\varepsilon}(x)-v_{\sigma}(x)\right)\right)(x)\geq 0
if
x\in\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
. Hence, using the maximum principle for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}
we conclude
|u^{\varepsilon}-v_{\sigma}|\leq K\psi\leq CK_{\varepsilon}
for
x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
and for some constant
C(\Omega,K,g_{\nu})
since
\psi
is bounded (cf. Theorem
4.2
). Thus,

CK_{\varepsilon}-v_{\sigma}(x)\leq u^{\varepsilon}(x)\leq CK_{\varepsilon}+v_{\sigma}(x)

(4.92)

for all
x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
. Since
\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}K_{\varepsilon}=C\left(\omega(\sigma)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)
, we obtain

C\left(\omega(\sigma)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)-v_{\sigma}(x)=\liminf\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}u^{\varepsilon}(x)\leq\limsup\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}u^{\varepsilon}(x)=C\left(\omega(\sigma)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+v_{\sigma}(x).

Letting first
L\to\infty
and then
\sigma\to 0
we conclude

v(x)=\liminf\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}u^{\varepsilon}(x)\leq\limsup\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}u^{\varepsilon}(x)=v(x)

and hence

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}u^{\varepsilon}(x)=v(x)

for all
x\in\Omega
.
The convergence is not only pointwise but also uniform in every compact set. Let indeed
A\subset\Omega
be compact. Then there exists an
\varepsilon_{0}
such that
A\subset\Omega_{\varepsilon}
for all
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
. Since
CK_{\varepsilon}
is independent of
x\in\Omega
and
|v-v_{\sigma}|\leq\omega(\sigma)
uniformly in
x\in\Omega
equation (
4.92
) implies

\sup\limits_{x\in A}|u^{\varepsilon}(x)-v(x)|\leq CK_{\varepsilon}+\omega(\sigma)\overset{\varepsilon\to 0,L\to\infty,\sigma\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0

Hence, Theorem
4.3
follows.
∎

5
Diffusion approximation for space dependent absorption coefficient

We could prove in the previous sections the convergence of the initial boundary value problem (
1.5
) to the solution of the Laplacian for constant absorption coefficient. In this section we will show an analogous result for the case, when the absorption coefficient depends on
x\in\Omega
, but it does not depend on the frequency.

5.1
The limit problem and the boundary layer equation

We assume, as we did throughout the paper,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
to be a convex bounded domain with
C^{3}
-boundary and strictly positive curvature. From now on we also assume
\alpha\in C^{3}(\overline{\Omega})
with
0<c_{0}\leq\alpha(x)\leq\Arrowvert\alpha\Arrowvert_{C^{3}}:=c_{1}<\infty
. We define
\overline{\alpha}\in C_{b}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
to be the extension of
\alpha
in the whole space with
0<c_{0}\leq\overline{\alpha}(x)\leq c_{1}
and
\overline{\alpha}\left.\right|_{\Omega}=\alpha
. For conivience we will denote
\overline{\alpha}
by
\alpha
. We assume
g_{\nu}
to satisfy the same assumption as in the rest of the paper, namely
g_{\nu}(n)\geq 0
with
\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{\nu}(n)\;d\nu\in L^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{S}^{2}\right)
. We study the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
of the following boundary value problem

\begin{cases}n\cdot\nabla_{x}I_{\nu}\left(x,n\right)=\frac{\alpha(x)}{\varepsilon}\left(B_{\nu}\left(T\left(x\right)\right)-I_{\nu}\left(x,n\right)\right)&x\in\Omega,\\
\nabla_{x}\cdot\mathcal{F}=0&x\in\Omega,\\
I_{\nu}\left(x,n\right)=g_{\nu}\left(n\right)&x\in\partial\Omega\text{ and }n\cdot N_{x}<0.\end{cases}

(5.1)

We proceed in the same way as in the case of constant absorption coefficient. Following the computation in Section 2.1 we obtain the limit problem in the interior
\Omega
for
u(x)=4\pi\sigma T^{4}(x)
as the elliptic equation

-\Div\left(\frac{1}{\alpha(x)}\nabla_{x}u(x)\right)=0.

(5.2)

For the boundary layer equation we argue similarly as in Section 2.2. Let
x_{0}\in\partial\Omega
and let
\mathcal{R}_{x_{0}}
be the rigid motion in (
1.12
). We rescale
x=\frac{\varepsilon}{\alpha(x_{0})}\mathcal{R}_{x_{0}}^{-1}(y)+x_{0}
for
y\in\frac{\alpha(x_{0})}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{R}_{x_{0}}\left(\Omega\right)
and we define
\overline{g}_{\nu}(n)=g_{\nu}\left(\mathcal{R}_{x_{0}}^{-1}(n)\right)
. Moreover, since
\alpha
is a
C^{3}
-function we also have for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small that
\alpha(x)=\alpha(x_{0})+\frac{\varepsilon}{\alpha(x_{0})}\mathcal{O}(|y|)
, and hence taking
\varepsilon\to 0
we obtain once again for
N=\mathcal{R}_{x_{0}}(N_{x_{0}})

\begin{cases}n\cdot\nabla_{y}I_{\nu}\left(y,n\right)=\left(B_{\nu}\left(T\left(y\right)\right)-I_{\nu}\left(y,n\right)\right)&y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
\nabla_{y}\cdot\mathcal{F}=0&y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
I_{\nu}\left(y,n\right)=\overline{g}_{\nu}\left(n\right)&y\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ and }n\cdot N<0.\end{cases}

This implies, that the boundary layer equation for
\overline{u}(y_{1},p)=4\pi\sigma T^{4}(y)
is also in this case given by the integral equation (
2.8
)

\overline{u}(y_{1},p)-\int_{0}^{\infty}d\eta\;K(y_{1}-\eta)u(\eta,p)=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\int_{n\cdot N_{p}<0}dn\;g_{\nu}(n)e^{-\frac{y_{1}}{\left|n\cdot N_{p}\right|}},

where
K
is the normalized exponential integral.
Hence, the whole theory developed in Section 3 is still valid and can be summarized by the Proposition (
3.1
).

Before moving to the rigorous proof of the convergence to the solution of the elliptic equation given in (
5.2
) we remark that the function
\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p):=\overline{u}\left(\frac{\alpha(p)}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{1},p\right)
solves the integral equation

\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)-\int_{\Pi_{p}}d\eta\;\frac{\alpha(p)e^{-\frac{\alpha(p)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(\eta,p)=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\int_{n\cdot N_{p}<0}dn\;g_{\nu}(n)e^{-\frac{\alpha(p)\left|x-x_{\Pi_{p}}(x,n)\right|}{\varepsilon}}.

(5.3)

5.2
Rigorous proof of the convergence: equation for
u^{\varepsilon}
and properties of the kernel

We can now move to the proof of the convergence of the solution to the problem (
5.1
) to the elliptic equation (
5.2
). We will follow all arguments given in Section 4 and change them were needed. Hence, first of all we find the integral equation that the sequence
u^{\varepsilon}(x)=4\pi\sigma T_{\varepsilon}^{4}(x)
associated to the solution
I^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(x,n)
of (
5.1
) satisfies. We follow the computations of Section 4.1. Let
x\in\Omega
,
n\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
x_{\Omega}(x,n)\in\partial\Omega
the unique intersection point of the line
\{x-tn:t>0\}
with the boundary. Let
s(x,n)=|x-x_{\Omega}(x,n)|
and let us denote for
x,z\in\overline{\Omega}
by
\int_{[x,z]}\alpha(\xi)\;ds(\xi)
the integral along the line connecting
x
with
z
, i.e.
\int_{\left[x,x_{\Omega}(x,n)\right]}\alpha(\xi)\;ds(\xi)=\int_{0}^{s(x,n)}\alpha(x-tn)\;dt
. Solving the first equation in (
5.1
) by characteristics we obtain

I^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}(x,n)=g_{\nu}(n)e^{-\int_{\left[x,x_{\Omega}(x,n)\right]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}\;ds(\xi)}+\int_{0}^{s(x,n)}e^{-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\alpha(x-\tau n)}{\varepsilon}d\tau}\frac{\alpha(x-tn)}{\varepsilon}B_{\nu}\left(T\left({x-tn}\right)\right)\;dt.

Therefore, analogously as in Section 4.1 using that the flux is divergence free together with the first equation in (
5.1
), equation (
1.3
), the characteristic solution of
I^{\varepsilon}
and changing from spherical coordinates to space coordinates we obtain the following integral equation for
u^{\varepsilon}(x)=4\pi\sigma T_{\varepsilon}^{4}(x)

u^{\varepsilon}(x)-\int_{\Omega}\frac{\alpha(\eta)e^{-\int_{[x,\eta]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}u^{\varepsilon}(\eta)\;d\eta=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}dn\;g_{\nu}(n)e^{-\int_{\left[x,x_{\Omega}(x,n)\right]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}\;ds(\xi)}.

(5.4)

For
x,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we define the kernel
K_{\varepsilon}
by

K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)=\frac{\alpha(\eta)e^{-\int_{[x,\eta]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}.

(5.5)

Notice that
K_{\varepsilon}
has been defined in the whole
\mathbb{R}^{3}
extending
\alpha
by
\overline{\alpha}
. We remark that
K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)
is not symmetric, i.e.
K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\neq K_{\varepsilon}(\eta;x)
.
In the following we summarize some properties of the kernel
K_{\varepsilon}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

The kernel
K_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
5.5
) has integral equal
1
. Moreover, it can be decomposed in the following three different ways:

(i)

Let
x,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, then
K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)=K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+R^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)
, where
K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)=\frac{\alpha(x)e^{-\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}
. Moreover, the remainder satisfies

\left|R^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\right|\leq C(c_{0},c_{1})\frac{c_{0}e^{-\frac{c_{0}|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}\begin{cases}\left(|x-\eta|+\frac{|x-\eta|^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)&|x-\eta|<\sqrt{\varepsilon}\\
1&|x-\eta|\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

(ii)

Let
x,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
p\in\partial\Omega
with
|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}
for
\delta>0
very small. Then
K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)=K^{\alpha(p)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+R^{p}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)
, where the remainder satisfies

\hskip-19.91684pt\left|R^{p}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\right|\leq C(c_{0},c_{1})\frac{c_{0}e^{-\frac{c_{0}|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}\begin{cases}\left(|x-p|+|x-\eta|+\frac{|x-\eta|^{2}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}|x-p|\right)&|x-\eta|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}\\
1&|x-\eta|\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}.\end{cases}

(iii)

Let
x,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, then
K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)=K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}^{2}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+\tilde{R}^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)
, where

\mathcal{K}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\alpha(x)e^{-\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot(\eta-x)\frac{|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}

and

\mathcal{K}^{2}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)=\frac{e^{-\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot(\eta-x).

Moreover, the remainder satisfies

\left|\tilde{R}^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\right|\leq C(c_{0},c_{1})\begin{cases}\frac{c_{0}e^{-\frac{c_{0}|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}\left(\frac{|x-\eta|^{3}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{|x-\eta|^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)&|x-\eta|<\sqrt{\varepsilon}\\
\frac{c_{0}e^{-\frac{c_{0}|x-\eta|}{2\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}(1+\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2})&|x-\eta|\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

(5.6)

Notice that (iii) is a refinement of (i).

Proof.

We start proving that the integral of
K_{\varepsilon}
is
1
. We compute changing to spherical coordinates as
\eta=x-rn

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}&\frac{\alpha(\eta)e^{-\int_{[x,\eta]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}\;d\eta=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}dn\int_{0}^{\infty}dr\;\frac{\alpha(x-rn)}{4\pi\varepsilon}e^{-\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\alpha(x-tn)}{\varepsilon}dt}\\
&=-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}dn\int_{0}^{\infty}dr\;\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{d}{dr}e^{-\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\alpha(x-tn)}{\varepsilon}dt}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}dn\;\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(1-e^{-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha(x-tn)}{\varepsilon}dt}\right)=1,\end{split}

since
\alpha\geq c_{0}>0
.

We now proceed with the decompositions of the kernel. We start with claim (i). To this end we consider first of all
|x-\eta|<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. We can expand by Taylor and get

\alpha(\eta)=\alpha(x)+\mathcal{O}\left(|x-\eta|\right)\;\;\;\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\;\;\;\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\alpha(x-\tau(x-\eta))d\tau|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}=\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|x-\eta|^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right).

(5.7)

Since
|x-\eta|<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
we can conclude

e^{-\int_{[x,\eta]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)}=e^{-\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|x-\eta|^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).

(5.8)

By assumptions
c_{0}\leq\alpha(x)\leq c_{1}
and hence (
5.7
) and (
5.8
) imply claim (i) in the case
|x-\eta|<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. In the case
|x-\eta|\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
we use the rough estimate

\left|\alpha(\eta)e^{-\int_{[x,\eta]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)}-\alpha(x)e^{-\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}\right|\leq 2c_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{0}|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}.

(5.9)

Concerning claim (ii) we argue similarly. Let
p\in\partial\Omega
and
|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}
. Let us first of all consider
|x-\eta|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}
, then
|\eta-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. We expand
\alpha
again by Taylor around
p
. Hence, similarly as before

\alpha(\eta)=\alpha(p)+\mathcal{O}\left(|\eta-p|\right)\;\text{ and }\;e^{-\int_{[x,\eta]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)}=e^{-\frac{\alpha(p)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|x-\eta|^{2}+|x-p||x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).

(5.10)

Using on one hand
|\eta-p|\leq|\eta-x|+|x-p|
and on the other hand a rough estimate as in (
5.9
) we conclude also the proof of claim (ii).

It remains to show claim (iii). We assume again first of all
|x-\eta|<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. We expand
\alpha
using Taylor. Since
\alpha\in C_{b}^{3}\left(\mathbb{R}^{3}\right)
all terms in the computation below are well-defined. Hence, we see

\alpha(\eta)=\alpha(x)+\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot(\eta-x)+\mathcal{O}\left(|x-\eta|^{2}\right)

(5.11)

and

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\alpha(x-\tau(x-\eta))d\tau|x-\eta|=&\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\alpha(x)+\tau\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot(\eta-x)+\mathcal{O}\left(|x-\eta|^{2}\right)d\tau|x-\eta|\\
=&\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot(\eta-x)\frac{|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|x-\eta|^{3}}{\varepsilon}\right).\end{split}

Thus, this implies for
|x-\eta|<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
the estimate

e^{-\int_{[x,\eta]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)}=e^{-\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot(\eta-x)\frac{|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|x-\eta|^{3}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{|x-\eta|^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\right).

(5.12)

Equations (
5.11
) and (
5.12
) imply the decomposition
K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)=K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}^{2}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+\tilde{R}^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)
and the estimate on
\tilde{R}^{x}_{\varepsilon}
when
|x-\eta|<\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, while a rough estimate similar to (
5.9
) and the well-known inequality
e^{-|x|}|x|^{n}\leq C_{n}e^{-\frac{|x|}{2}}
imply the claim (iii) for
|x-\eta|\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
.
∎

This proposition is one of the key results which will allow us to generalize the results obtained in the previous section when the absorption coefficient depends smoothly enough on the space variable. There are two very important consequences. First of all, the Banach fixed point theorem guarantees a unique continuous bounded solution
u^{\varepsilon}
. Indeed, the continuity of the function
\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}
and its integrability in
\Omega
together with the uniform continuity in
x
and uniform boundedness of
e^{-\int_{[x,\eta]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)}
imply that the operator
A_{g}^{\varepsilon}:C(\Omega)\to C(\Omega)
is a selfmap, where we consider

A_{g}^{\varepsilon}(u)(x)=\int_{\Omega}d\eta K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)u(\eta)+\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}dn\;g_{\nu}(n)e^{-\int_{[x,x_{\Omega}(x,n)]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)}.

Moreover, since the integral on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of the kernel
K_{\varepsilon}
is
1
, arguing as in Section 4.1 we conclude that
A_{g}^{\varepsilon}
is a contraction.

The second consequence is that we can prove for the integral operator
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(u)(x):=u(x)-\int_{\Omega}d\eta\;K_{\varepsilon}(x;n)u(\eta)
comparison properties identical to the one in Theorem
4.1
(Maximum principle). Hence,
u^{\varepsilon}
in (
5.4
) is non-negative.

5.3
Rigorous proof of the convergence: uniform boundedness of
u^{\varepsilon}

Next we generalize Section 4.2 for the case
\alpha(x)\in C^{3}(\overline{\Omega})
. We want to show that the sequence
u^{\varepsilon}
is uniform bounded in
\varepsilon
. As we have seen in Section 5.2 the maximum principle of Theorem
4.1
holds. We want to apply it with a suitable supersolution. Indeed, using the notation
\varepsilon d_{\varepsilon}\penalty\ =\penalty\ d(x)
as in Lemma
4.2
we see
\int_{[x,x_{\Omega}(x,n)]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}ds(\xi)\geq\frac{c_{0}|x-x_{\Omega}|}{\varepsilon}\geq c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}(x)
and thus
\left|\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}\left(u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\right|\leq\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{1}e^{-c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}(x)}
.

The supersolution we will consider is similar to the one constructed in Theorem
4.2
. However, it is now not true anymore that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(C-|x|^{2})\geq 2\varepsilon^{2}
for every
x\in\Omega
. This can be already been looking at the expected limit elliptic operator
L=-\Div\left(\frac{1}{\alpha(x)}\nabla_{x}\right)
, for which the inequality
L\left(C-|x|^{2}\right)\geq 0
holds for arbitrary functions
\alpha
depending on
x
only for small values of
|x|
. We remark that the supersolution constructed in Theorem
4.2
contains two parts, namely a multiple of
C-|x|^{2}
and a function proportional to the function
\psi
constructed in Lemma
4.2
. The role of the function
\psi
is to control the value of the operator
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}
near the boundary
\partial\Omega
. The contribution of
\psi
is relevant only in the region where
e^{-d_{\varepsilon}(x)}\geq\varepsilon^{2}
. We will see that the function
\psi
still gives a supersolution for the new operator
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}
(up to an error of order
\varepsilon^{2}
). Therefore, in order to prove the analogous of Theorem
4.2
we need to replace the function
C-|x|^{2}
by exponential functions.

Theorem 5.1
.

There exist suitable constants
0<\mu<1
,
0<\gamma(\mu)<\frac{1}{3}
,
C_{1},\;C_{2},\;C_{3}>0
,
\lambda>0
large enough and there exists some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that the function

\Phi^{\varepsilon}(x)=C_{3}\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{1}\left[\left(e^{\lambda D}+C_{1}-e^{\lambda x_{1}}\right)+C_{2}\left(\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{1+\left(\frac{c_{0}d(x)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\right)\wedge\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{1+\left(\frac{c_{0}\mu R}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\right)\right)\right],

for
a\wedge b\>=\min\left(a,b\right)
,
R>0
the minimal radius of curvature,
D=\textnormal{diam}(\Omega)
and
d(x):=\text{dist}\left(x,\partial\Omega\right)
,
satisfies
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(\Phi^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\geq\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{0}d(x)}{\varepsilon}}
in
\Omega
uniformly for all
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
. Moreover, the solutions
u^{\varepsilon}
of (
5.4
) are uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon
.

Proof.

We will use the notation of Theorem
4.2
and Lemma
4.1
and
4.2
. Notice first of all that

\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}K^{c_{1}}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)\leq K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\leq\frac{c_{1}}{c_{0}}K^{c_{0}}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta),

(5.13)

where we used the notation of Proposition
5.1
. Moreover, using the decomposition of claim (i) in Proposition
5.1
we can always estimate for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
the integral
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|R^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)||(\eta-x)|^{n}\;d\eta
by

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|R^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)||(\eta-x)|^{n}\;d\eta\leq&C(c_{0},c_{1})\int_{|x-\eta|<\sqrt{\varepsilon}}K_{\varepsilon}^{c_{0}}(x-\eta)\left(\frac{|\eta-x|^{2+n}}{\varepsilon}+|x-\eta|^{1+n}\right)\;|d\eta\\
&+C(c_{0},c_{1})\int_{|x-\eta|\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}}K_{\varepsilon}^{c_{0}}(x-\eta)|(\eta-x)|^{n}\;d\eta\\
\leq&C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{n+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-r}r^{n+1}(1+r)\;dr+C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{n}\int_{\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}^{\infty}e^{-r}r^{n}\;dr\\
\leq&C(c_{0},c_{1},n)\varepsilon^{n+1}+C(c_{0},c_{1},n)\varepsilon^{n}e^{-\frac{c_{0}}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}\leq C(c_{0},c_{1},n)\varepsilon^{n+1},\end{split}

(5.14)

where we used
e^{-|x|}|x|^{m}\leq C_{m}
.
Let now
x\in\Omega
. Let
x_{0}\in\partial\Omega
be a point such that
|x-x_{0}|=d(x)
. Then we estimate with the help of Proposition
5.1

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(1)=&\int_{\Omega^{c}}d\eta\;K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\geq\int_{\Pi_{x_{0}}}d\eta\;K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{-}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}K^{c_{1}}_{\varepsilon}(d(x)e_{1}-\eta)\\
=&\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\int_{-\infty}^{-c_{1}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}K(y)\;dy\geq\begin{cases}\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\nu_{c_{1}M_{0}}&d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)\leq M_{0},\\
0&\forall x\in\Omega.\end{cases}\end{split}

This estimate will play a crucial role the proof. We proceed considering the function
\varphi(x)=e^{\lambda D}-e^{\lambda x_{1}}
. It is not difficult to see that for
x\in\Omega
we get
\varphi(x)\geq 0
. Moreover, expanding by Taylor
e^{\lambda\eta_{1}}=e^{\lambda x_{1}}+\lambda e^{\lambda x_{1}}(\eta_{1}-x_{1})+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}(\eta_{1}-x_{1})^{2}+E_{3}(x;\eta)
with
|E_{3}(x;\eta)|\leq\frac{\lambda^{3}}{6}e^{\lambda D}|x_{1}-\eta_{1}|^{3}
we compute

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}&\left(e^{\lambda x_{1}}\right)=e^{\lambda x_{1}}-\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)e^{\lambda\eta_{1}}d\eta\\
=&e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\Omega^{c}}K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\;d\eta-\lambda e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)(\eta_{1}-x_{1})\;d\eta\\
&-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)(\eta_{1}-x_{1})^{2}\;d\eta-\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)E_{3}(x;\eta)\;d\eta.\end{split}

(5.15)

The first term on the right hand side can be controlled by
e^{\lambda D}
. For the second term we use the decomposition of claim (i) in Proposition
5.1
, the symmetry of the operator
K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}
and the estimate (
5.14
). Moreover, we remind that
\Omega^{c}\subset B^{c}_{d(x)}(x)
. Hence,

\begin{split}-\lambda e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)(\eta_{1}-x_{1})\;d\eta=&\lambda e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\Omega^{c}}K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)(\eta_{1}-x_{1})\;d\eta-\lambda e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\Omega}R_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)(\eta_{1}-x_{1})\;d\eta\\
\leq&\lambda\frac{c_{1}}{c_{0}^{2}}\varepsilon e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{2}}e^{\lambda x_{1}}+C(c_{0},c_{1})\lambda e^{\lambda x_{1}}\varepsilon^{2}.\end{split}

(5.16)

For the third term in equation (
5.15
) we can proceed as follows using the decomposition of claim (i) of Proposition
5.1
and (
5.14
)

\begin{split}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\Omega}&K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)(\eta_{1}-x_{1})^{2}\;d\eta=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)(\eta_{1}-x_{1})^{2}\;d\eta\\
&-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\Omega^{c}}K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)(\eta_{1}-x_{1})^{2}\;d\eta+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}\int_{\Omega}R_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)(\eta_{1}-x_{1})^{2}\;d\eta\\
\geq&\frac{2\varepsilon^{2}}{3c_{1}^{2}}\lambda^{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{c_{0}^{2}}\lambda^{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{2}}-c(c_{0},c_{1})\lambda^{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}\varepsilon^{3}.\end{split}

(5.17)

Finally, using the estimate (
5.13
) for
K_{\varepsilon}
we compute for the term containing the error
E_{3}(x;\eta)

\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)E_{3}(x;\eta)\;d\eta\leq\lambda^{3}e^{\lambda D}C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-r}r^{3}\leq C(c_{0},c_{1})\lambda^{3}e^{\lambda D}C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{3}.

(5.18)

Hence, (
5.16
), (
5.17
) and (
5.18
) imply for
\lambda>0
large enough and
0<\varepsilon<1
sufficiently small

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}\left(e^{\lambda D}-e^{\lambda x_{1}}\right)\geq&\frac{2\varepsilon^{2}}{3c_{1}^{2}}\lambda^{2}e^{\lambda x_{1}}-C(c_{0},c_{1})\lambda e^{\lambda x_{1}}\left(\varepsilon e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{2}}+\varepsilon^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\lambda e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{2}}\right)-c(c_{0},c_{1},\lambda,D)\varepsilon^{3}\\
\geq&\begin{cases}-A_{1}\varepsilon e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{2}}&\text{ if }d(x)<\frac{2\varepsilon}{c_{0}}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right),\\
A_{2}\varepsilon^{2}&\text{ if }d(x)\geq\frac{2\varepsilon}{c_{0}}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right),\end{cases}\end{split}

where
A_{1}(c_{0},c_{1},\lambda,D)>0
and
A_{2}(c_{0},c_{1},\lambda,D)>0
are constants independent of
\varepsilon
.

We proceed with the estimate for the operator acting on
\psi(x)=\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{1+\left(\frac{c_{0}d(x)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\right)\wedge\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{1+\left(\frac{c_{0}\mu R}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\right)
. Arguing similarly as in Lemma
4.2
we can show that for
\mu>0
and
0<\gamma<\frac{1}{3}
small enough and
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}<\frac{R\mu^{3}}{2}
sufficiently small there exists constants
A_{3}(R,\Omega,\mu,\gamma)>0
and
A_{4}(R,\mu,\gamma)>0
such that

\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(\psi)(x)\geq\begin{cases}A_{3}\frac{1}{\left(1+(c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right))^{2}\right)^{2}}&\text{ if }0<d(x)\leq\frac{R\mu}{2},\\
-A_{4}\varepsilon^{2}&\text{ if }\frac{R\mu}{2}<d(x)<R\mu,\\
0&\text{ if }d(x)\geq R\mu.\end{cases}

The proof works following the same steps of Lemma
4.2
. Indeed, for the regions
\{d(x)\geq R\mu\}
and
\{0<d(x)<M\varepsilon\}
, for
M=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}
, the proof does not change. We use the estimate (
5.13
) for
K_{\varepsilon}
and the fact that its integral in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
1
. The constant
\gamma
must be chosen sufficiently small so that
\gamma<\frac{\nu_{c_{1}M}c_{0}}{2c_{1}}
. For the regions
\{M\varepsilon\leq d(x)\leq\frac{R\mu}{2}\}
and
\{\frac{R\mu}{2}<d(x)<R\mu\}
we use again the Taylor expansion, the estimates on
K_{\varepsilon}
, the fact that
\varepsilon<\frac{R\mu^{2}}{2}
and
M=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}
. Beside the fact that
K_{\varepsilon}
has integral
1
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
we use its decomposition
K_{\varepsilon}=K_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha(x)}+R^{x}_{\varepsilon}
according to claim (i) in Proposition
5.1
and the estimate (
5.14
) for the remainder. In every computation where we used the symmetry of the kernel, e.g. for the first term of the Taylor expansion, we decompose
K_{\varepsilon}
and apply the symmetry argument for
K_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha(x)}
and estimate the remainder. We omit the details of the proof, since the computations are similar to those in Lemma
4.2
.

We now finish the proof of Theorem
5.1
. Let
\varepsilon_{0}\leq\varepsilon_{1}
such that
\frac{R\mu}{2}>\frac{2\varepsilon}{c_{0}}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
. Let
C_{2}=\frac{A_{2}}{2A_{4}}
. Moreover, since
(1+x^{2})^{2}e^{-\frac{x}{2}}\to 0
as
x\to\infty
there exists some
M_{0}>0
such that if
d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)\geq M_{0}
then

-A_{1}\varepsilon e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{2}}+\frac{C_{2}A_{3}}{\left(1+(c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right))^{2}\right)^{2}}\geq\frac{C_{2}A_{3}}{2\left(1+(c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right))^{2}\right)^{2}}\geq\frac{C_{2}A_{3}}{12}e^{-c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}.

Let
C_{1}>0
satisfy
C_{1}>\frac{A_{1}c_{1}}{c_{0}\nu_{c_{1}M_{0}}}
. Then for all
d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)<M_{0}
we obtain

\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(C_{1})\geq C_{1}\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\nu_{c_{1}M_{0}}>A_{1}\varepsilon e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{2}}.

Finally, taking
C_{3}^{-1}=\min\{\frac{A_{2}}{2},\frac{C_{2}A_{3}}{12}\}
we get the desired lower estimate
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}\left(\Phi^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\geq\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{0}d(x)}{\varepsilon}}
.

We conclude the proof of this Theorem applying the maximum principle of Theorem
4.1
to the continuous function
\Phi^{\varepsilon}-u^{\varepsilon}
.

∎

Remark
.

We notice for further reference that we have obtained a stronger estimate than
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}\left(\Phi^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\geq\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{0}d(x)}{\varepsilon}}
, namely

\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}\left(\Phi^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\geq\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{1}\begin{cases}\frac{6}{\left(1+(c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(\varepsilon\right))^{2}\right)^{2}}&\text{ if }d(x)<\frac{2\varepsilon}{c_{0}}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\\
\varepsilon^{2}+\frac{6}{\left(1+(c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(\varepsilon\right))^{2}\right)^{2}}&\text{ if }\frac{2\varepsilon}{c_{0}}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\leq d(x)<\frac{R\mu}{2}\\
\varepsilon^{2}&\text{ if }d(x)\geq\frac{R\mu}{2}\end{cases}\geq\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{1}\varepsilon^{2},

(5.19)

since
\frac{6}{\left(1+(c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(\varepsilon\right))^{2}\right)^{2}}\geq e^{-\frac{c_{0}d(x)}{\varepsilon}}\geq\varepsilon^{2}
if
d(x)<\frac{2\varepsilon}{c_{0}}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
. This estimate will be important at the end of the paper.

5.4
Rigorous proof of the convergence: estimates of
u^{\varepsilon}-\overline{u}
near the boundary
\partial\Omega

In this section we will extend the results of Section 4.3 also for the case of space dependent absorption coefficient. We will show a slightly different result than the one in Section 4.3. We will prove that
\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)
as defined in (
5.3
) is a good approximation of
u^{\varepsilon}(x)
in a neighborhood of
p\in\partial\Omega
of size close to
\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}
istead of
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
. We will use once more the maximum principle of Theorem
4.1
. We start with the estimate of
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}\left(\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,p)-u^{\varepsilon}\right)
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
p\in\partial\Omega
and let
\mathcal{R}_{p}
be the rigid motion defined in (
1.12
). Then the following holds for
x\in\Omega
,
\delta>0
sufficiently small and independent of
\varepsilon
and a suitable
0<A<\frac{1}{4}
and constant
C>0

\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{u}\left(\alpha(p)\frac{\mathcal{R}_{p}(\cdot)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},p\right)-u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\right|\leq Ce^{-Ac_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}\begin{cases}1,&\forall x\in\Omega\\
\varepsilon^{\delta}&\text{ if }|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}.\end{cases}

(5.20)

Proof.

We start with the rough estimate for
x\in\Omega
. The operator can be estimated by

\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right)\right|\leq\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)\right)\right|+\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right)\right|.

As we have seen in Section 5.3 it is always true that
\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right)\right|\leq\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{1}e^{-c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}
. Hence, we have to consider
\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)=\overline{u}_{\infty}(p)+\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)
with
\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)=\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)-\overline{u}_{\infty}(p)
and thus by Lemma
3.4

\left|\overline{V}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)\right|\leq Ce^{-\frac{c_{0}|\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{1}|}{2\varepsilon}}
.

By the geometry of the problem we can estimate
|\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{1}|\geq d(x)
. Indeed, let
x_{p}\in\partial\Omega
be the unique intersection point of the line
\{x+tN_{p}:t>0\}
with the boundary
\partial\Omega
, i.e.
x_{p}=x+t_{p}N_{p}
. Then, since
\Omega
is convex,

|\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{1}|=(x-p)\cdot(-N_{p})\geq(x-x_{p})\cdot(-N_{p})=t_{p}=|x-x_{p}|\geq d(x).

(5.21)

Hence, using also that
|x-\eta|\geq|d(\eta)-d(x)|
we compute

\begin{split}\left|\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}\right.&\left.\left(\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)\right)\right|\leq\overline{u}_{\infty}(p)\int_{\Omega^{c}}K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)d\eta+Ce^{-\frac{c_{0}}{2}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}+C\int_{\Omega}K^{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)e^{-\frac{c_{0}}{2}d_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta\right)}\;d\eta\\
\leq&C(c_{0},c_{1})\Arrowvert\overline{u}_{\infty}\Arrowvert_{\infty}e^{-\frac{c_{0}}{2}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{r}{2}}\;dr+Ce^{-\frac{c_{0}}{2}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}\\
&+C(c_{0},c_{1})\int_{d(\eta)<d(x)}\frac{e^{-\frac{c_{0}|x-\eta|}{2\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}e^{-\frac{c_{0}}{2}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}\;d\eta+Ce^{-\frac{c_{0}}{2}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}\int_{d(\eta)\geq d(x)}K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\;d\eta\\
\leq&C(c_{0},c_{1},\Arrowvert\overline{u}_{\infty}\Arrowvert_{\infty})e^{-\frac{c_{0}}{2}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}.\end{split}

We now prove the estimate when
|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}
. In this case we use the decomposition
K_{\varepsilon}=K^{\alpha(p)}_{\varepsilon}+R_{\varepsilon}^{p}
in claim (ii) of Proposition
5.1
. Similarly as in (
5.14
) we can estimate

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|R^{p}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)||x-\eta|^{n}\;d\eta\leq C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{n}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}.

(5.22)

Hence, using the decomposition above we compute for
|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}

\begin{split}\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\right.&\left.\left(\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right)\right|\\
\leq&\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}dn\;g_{\nu}(n)e^{-\int_{\left[x,x_{\Omega}(x,n)\right]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}\;ds(\xi)}-\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\int_{n\cdot N_{p}<0}dn\;g_{\nu}(n)e^{-\frac{\alpha(p)\left|x-x_{\Pi_{p}}(x,n)\right|}{\varepsilon}}\right|\\
&+\int_{\Pi_{p}\setminus\Omega}d\eta\;K_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha(p)}(x-p)\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(\eta,p)+\int_{\Omega}d\eta\;|R^{p}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)|\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(\eta)\\
=&I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.\end{split}

We estimate now these three terms. Analogous to Lemma
4.3
we decompose
\mathbb{S}^{2}=\mathcal{U}_{1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{2}\cup\mathcal{U}_{3}
. For the term
I_{1}
we only have to notice that if
n\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
then
|x_{\Omega}-x|\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}(C(\Omega)\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}+1+\varepsilon^{\delta})\leq 2\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. Hence,
\frac{|\xi-p||x-x_{\Omega}|}{\varepsilon}<4\varepsilon^{2\delta}<1
for any
\xi=x-t(x-x_{\Omega})
,
0\leq t\leq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. Thus, we obtain using the Taylor expansion on
\alpha(\xi)
as we did in (
5.10
)

\begin{split}I_{1}\left.\right|_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}\leq&\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\int_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}dn\;g_{\nu}(n)\left|e^{-\frac{\alpha(p)|x-x_{\Omega}(x,n)|}{\varepsilon}}-e^{-\frac{\alpha(p)\left|x-x_{\Pi_{p}}(x,n)\right|}{\varepsilon}}\right|\\
&+\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\int_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}dn\;g_{\nu}(n)\left|e^{-\int_{\left[x,x_{\Omega}(x,n)\right]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}\;ds(\xi)}-e^{-\frac{\alpha(p)|x-x_{\Omega}(x,n)|}{\varepsilon}}\right|\\
\leq&C(\Omega)\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{\infty}\varepsilon^{\delta}e^{-c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}+C\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}dn\;e^{-\frac{\alpha(p)|x-x_{\Omega}(x,n)|}{\varepsilon}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\leq C(\Omega)\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{\infty}\varepsilon^{\delta}e^{-c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)},\end{split}

where to estimate the first term in the first inequality we used Lemma
4.3
, specifically equation (
4.48
), and to estimate the second term we expanded
\alpha(\xi)
at
\xi=p
. To estimate the contributions in the regions
\mathcal{U}_{2}
and
\mathcal{U}_{3}
, the estimate
\int_{\left[x,x_{\Omega}(x,n)\right]}\frac{\alpha(\xi)}{\varepsilon}\;ds(\xi)\geq-c_{0}\frac{|x-x_{\Omega}(x,n)|}{\varepsilon}
together with the result of Lemma
4.3
implies the bound on
I_{1}
. The term
I_{2}
can be handled exactly as in Lemma
4.3
. Finally, for
I_{3}
we use the uniform boundedness of
\overline{u}(y,p)
and equation (
5.22
). This implies

\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right)\right|\leq C(\Omega,c_{0},c_{1},g)\varepsilon^{\delta}e^{-c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}+C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}.

We conclude by interpolation. Indeed, if
d(x)\leq\frac{4\varepsilon}{c_{0}}\ln\left(\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}-\delta}\right)
, then
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta}\leq\varepsilon^{\delta}e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{4}}
, while if
d(x)>\frac{4\varepsilon}{c_{0}}\ln\left(\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}-\delta}\right)
we use the global estimate to get
e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{2}}\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{4}}
.
∎

It only remains to adapt the supersolution
W_{\varepsilon,L}
of Proposition
4.2
. As we anticipated at the beginning of Section 5.4 we are going to prove that
\overline{u}
is a good approximation of
u^{\varepsilon}
in a neighborhood of
p\in\partial\Omega
of size close to
\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}
. First of all we notice that if
\delta<\frac{1}{12}
then
\frac{2}{3}>\frac{1}{2}+2\delta
and hence we have also that

\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{u}\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_{p}(\cdot)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},p\right)-u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\right|\leq Ce^{-Ac_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }|x-p|\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}\\
\varepsilon^{\delta}&\text{ if }|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}.\end{cases}

The result we prove is the following

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
p\in\partial\Omega
,
0<A<\frac{1}{4}
the constant of Lemma
5.1
. Let
L>0
large enough and
0<\varepsilon<1
sufficiently small. Let
0<\delta<\frac{1}{12}
. Then there exists a non negative continuous function
W_{\varepsilon,L}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\begin{cases}W_{\varepsilon,L}\geq C>0&\text{ for }\left|\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{i}\right|\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta};\\
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(W_{\varepsilon,L}\right)(x)\geq C\varepsilon^{\delta}e^{-\frac{Ad(x)}{\varepsilon}}&\text{ for }\left|\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{i}\right|<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta};\\
0\leq W_{\varepsilon,L}\leq C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)&\text{ for }\left|\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{i}\right|<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta},\end{cases}

for some constant
C>0
and
\alpha>0
.

This proposition implies arguing as in Section 4.3, the following corollary (see Corollary
4.1
).

Corollary 5.1
.

There exists a constant
C>0
, a large
L>0
and an
\alpha>0
independent of
x,p,\varepsilon
such that

\left|\overline{u}\left(\frac{R_{p}(\cdot)\cdot e_{1}}{\varepsilon},p\right)-u^{\varepsilon}\right|(x)\leq C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)

for all
|x-p|<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}
.

In order to adapt the supersolution
W_{\varepsilon,L}
of Proposition
4.2
in this case we start considering a new slightly different geometrical setting. Once more we denote for simplicity
x_{i}=\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{i}
. We define now for
i=2,3
the radii
\rho_{i}^{\pm}(x)=\sqrt{\left(x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon\right)^{2}+\left(x_{i}\pm\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta}\right)^{2}}
and the angles
\theta_{i}^{\pm}(x)
given by
\cos\left(\theta_{i}^{\pm}\right)=\frac{1}{\rho_{i}^{\pm}(x)}\left(x_{1}+\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon\right)
. We construct then the function
W_{\varepsilon,L}
using now these definitions for
\rho_{i}^{\pm}(x)
and
\theta_{i}^{\pm}(x)
analogously to Section 4.3

W_{\varepsilon,L}(x)=\sum_{i=2}^{3}\left(W^{+}_{i}(x)+W^{-}_{i}(x)\right)+\frac{\tilde{C}}{\sqrt{L}}\phi_{\frac{1}{8},\varepsilon}+C\varepsilon^{\delta}\phi_{A,\varepsilon},

(5.23)

where
\phi_{A,\varepsilon}=\Phi^{\frac{\varepsilon}{A}}
the supersolution defined in Theorem
5.1
,
C,\tilde{C}>0
some suitable constants and
W^{\pm}_{i}=F^{\pm}_{i}(x)+G^{\pm}_{i}(x)+H^{\pm}_{i}(x)
given by the auxiliary functions defined in (
4.53
),(
4.54
) and (
4.55
) adapted to the new geometrical setting. Analogously to Section 4.3 we define the following subsets of
\Omega
for
i=2,3
.

\mathcal{C}^{+}_{i}:=\left\{x\in\Omega:\;x_{i}\leq-\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta}\text{ or }|x_{i}|<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta},x_{1}\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta}\right\};

\mathcal{C}^{-}_{i}:=\left\{x\in\Omega:\;x_{i}\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta}\text{ or }|x_{i}|<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta},x_{1}\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta}\right\};

\mathcal{C}_{\delta}:=\left\{x\in\Omega:\;x_{1}<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta}\text{ and }|x_{i}|<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta}\text{ for }i=2,3\right\};

\mathcal{C}_{i,2\delta}:=\left\{x\in\Omega:\;|x_{i}|<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}\text{ and }x_{1}<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}\right\}.

Also Lemma
4.4
can be extended. We can prove using the geometrical setting above and the defined functions and sets the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.2
.

Assume
p\in\Omega
,
0<\varepsilon<1
,
L,\delta
as indicated in Proposition
5.2
. Let
x_{i}=\mathcal{R}_{p}(x)\cdot e_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
. Let
W^{\pm}_{i}
as above. Then there exist a constant
\alpha>0
depending only on
\delta
and a constant
C>0
depending on
\Omega
,
g_{\nu}
,
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
but independent of
\varepsilon
and
p
and suitable
b>0
and
L>0
such that for
i=2,3

\displaystyle W^{\pm}_{i}(x)\geq 0

in
\Omega
,

(5.24)

\displaystyle W^{\pm}_{i}(x)\geq\frac{\pi}{2}-\arctan(2)

in
\mathcal{C}^{\pm}_{i}
,

(5.25)

\displaystyle W^{\pm}_{i}(x)\leq C\varepsilon^{\alpha}

in
\mathcal{C}_{i,2\delta}
,

(5.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(W^{\pm}_{i}\right)(x)\geq-\frac{C}{\sqrt{L}}e^{-\frac{c_{0}d_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{8}}

in
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
.

(5.27)

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
4.4
it is enough to prove it for
W=W^{-}_{2}
. Again we consider
\rho=\rho_{2}^{-}
,
\theta=\theta_{2}^{-}
,
F=F^{-}_{2}
,
G=G^{-}_{2}
,
H=H^{-}_{2}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2\delta}=\mathcal{C}_{2,2\delta}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{2}
. We only have to show claim (
5.27
), since all other claims work exactly in the same way. The only thing that is needed is that
\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}=\varepsilon^{\delta}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta}=\varepsilon^{2\delta}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}
. In this case we have
\beta=\frac{1-12\delta}{3}>0
and
\varepsilon<\left(\frac{1}{L}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}
.

In order to prove (
5.27
) we follow the strategy of Lemma
4.4
. We expand hence by Taylor the function
W
putting together equations (
4.68
), (
4.69
) and (
4.70
). Exactly as in Section 4.3 we consider for
x\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
the three cases
\rho(x)<L\varepsilon
,
\rho(x)\geq L\varepsilon
with
d(x)<\varepsilon
and
\rho(x)\geq L\varepsilon
with
d(x)\geq\varepsilon
. For each of these situations the same estimates of the error term
E^{3}(\eta,x)
as in (
4.72
) holds. We substitute for
W(\eta)
in the formulation of
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{\Omega}(W)(x)
the Taylor expansion. For all terms containing the first, second and third derivatives of
W
we argued in Lemma
4.4
by the symmetry of the kernel. In this case is not possible anymore. Hence, for that terms, we decompose the kernel
K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)=K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+R_{\varepsilon}^{x}(x;\eta)
according to claim (i) of Proposition
5.1
. For
K_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha(x)}
we use the same arguments as in Section 4. For the terms with the remainder
R_{\varepsilon}^{x}
we estimate in a different way. We notice first of all that if
x\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
, then

\rho(x)\leq 2\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+\delta}<\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}

(5.28)

taking
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. Since
\rho>\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon
and
\cos(\theta(x))\rho(x)\geq\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon
we recall also that for
n\geq 1

\begin{split}\varepsilon^{n}\left|\nabla_{x}^{n}W(x)\right|\leq&C_{F}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}}{\rho^{n}(x)}+bC_{H}\frac{\varepsilon^{n+2}}{\rho^{n+2}(x)}+aC_{G}\frac{\varepsilon}{L^{n-\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{n}\rho(x)}.\end{split}

This implies, using the estimates (
5.14
) and (
5.28
) and
\rho>\frac{L}{2}\varepsilon
that

\begin{split}\left|\nabla_{x}^{n}W(x)\right|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;|R^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)||x-\eta|^{n}\leq&\frac{C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{2}}{\rho(x)}\left(\frac{C_{F}\varepsilon^{n-1}}{\rho^{n-1}}+bC_{H}\frac{\varepsilon^{n+1}}{\rho^{n+1}(x)}+\frac{aC_{G}}{L^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}\right)\\
=&\frac{C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}(x)}\left(\frac{C_{F}\varepsilon^{n-1}}{\rho^{n-1}}+bC_{H}\frac{\varepsilon^{n+1}}{\rho^{n+1}(x)}+\frac{aC_{G}}{L^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}\right)\varepsilon\left(\frac{\rho(x)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{3}\\
\leq&\frac{C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}(x)}\left(\frac{C_{F}\varepsilon^{n-1}}{\rho^{n-1}}+bC_{H}\frac{\varepsilon^{n+1}}{\rho^{n+1}(x)}+\frac{aC_{G}}{L^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}\right)\\
\leq&\frac{C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}(x)}\left(C_{F}+\frac{bC_{H}}{L^{2}}+\frac{aC_{G}}{\sqrt{L}}\right).\end{split}

Hence, since
\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}(x)}=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho(x)}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho(x)}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}
all arguments and estimates we had in the proof of Lemma
4.4
for the first case when
\rho(x)<L\varepsilon
can be obtained also for this function
W
and for
\alpha\in C^{3}\left(\Omega\right)
. For the other two cases, when
\rho(x)\geq L\varepsilon
with
d(x)<\varepsilon
or
d(x)\geq\varepsilon
we need also to add to the assumption
a>b
the assumption
a=2b
(or even
a=b+1
). Then the term coming from the integral
