-\left|\nabla_{x}^{n}G(x)\right|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;|R^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)||x-\eta|^{n}\geq-\frac{C(c_{0},c_{1})2bC_{G}\varepsilon^{4}}{\sqrt{L}\rho^{4}(x)}

can be always absorbed taking
L
large enough by the term coming from the Laplacian of
H
when integrating the second derivative term of the Taylor expansion with the kernel
K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}
, i.e. it is absorbed by
\frac{2b}{3\alpha^{2}(x)}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}(x)}\geq\frac{2b}{3c_{1}^{2}}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\rho^{4}(x)}
. The remaining arguments and computation are similar to the one in the proof of Lemma
4.4
.
We thus refer to the that proof which implies Lemma
5.2
.
∎

Arguing as in Section 4.3, Lemma
5.2
implies now Proposition
5.1
for the supersolution
W_{\varepsilon,L}
as given in equation (
5.23
). In this case we have to use Theorem
5.1
instead of Theorem
4.2
.

5.5
Rigorous proof of the convergence of
u^{\varepsilon}
to the solution of the new boundary value problem

We are now ready conclude the proof of the convergence of
u^{\varepsilon}
to the function
v
, solution of the boundary value problem

\begin{cases}-\Div\left(\frac{1}{\alpha(x)}\nabla_{x}v(x)\right)=0&x\in\Omega,\\
v(p)=\overline{u}_{\infty}(p)&p\in\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

(5.29)

To this end, we generalize Section 4.4 for the case
\alpha\in C^{3}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
. Again, we decompose
\Omega
in new regions. In this case though, their distance from the boundary will be of the order
\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}
. Since the results in this last section are analogous to those we obtained in Section 4.4 we use the same notation. Hence, we define in this case
\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{x\in\Omega:d(x)>\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}\right\}
,
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{x\in\Omega:\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+4\delta}<d(x)\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}\right\}
and their union
\Omega_{\varepsilon}=\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\cup\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. We also define for
0<\sigma<1
sufficiently small independent of
\varepsilon
the set
\Omega^{\sigma}:=\Omega\cup\left\{x\in\Omega^{c}:d(x)<\sigma\right\}
. Recall the continuous projection
\pi_{\partial\Omega}
as given in (
1.13
) and the estimate
\left|\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)}(x)\cdot e_{1}\right|\geq d(x)
as we have seen in (
5.21
). Then as in Lemma
4.5
we can prove the following result.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
sufficiently small,
C,\;\alpha,\;L,\;0<\delta<\frac{1}{12}
according to Corollary
5.1
. Then

\sup_{x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\left|\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}-4\delta}.

Proof.

We combine the estimate in (
5.21
) with Lemma
3.4
, we use the Corollary
5.1
, the fact that
0<\delta<\frac{1}{12}
and the following estimate

\sup_{x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\left|\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq\sup_{x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\left|\overline{u}_{\infty}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)-\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}\left(x,\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)\right|+\sup_{x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\left|\overline{U}_{\varepsilon}\left(x,\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|.

∎

Similarly to Section 4.4 we consider the function
v_{\sigma}
, solution to the boundary value problem

\begin{cases}-\Div\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\alpha}(x)}\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\right)=0&x\in\Omega^{\sigma},\\
v(x)=\overline{u}_{\infty}(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x))&x\in\partial\Omega^{\sigma},\end{cases}

(5.30)

where we consider the smooth extension of
\alpha
as defined at the beginning of Section 5.1. Since
\overline{\alpha}\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\pi_{\partial\Omega}
is a continuous bijection and
\overline{u}_{\infty}
is Lipschitz, the theory on elliptic regularity assures that
v_{\sigma}
uniquely exists and it is also three times continuously differentiable, i.e.
v_{\sigma}\in C^{3}\left(\Omega^{\sigma}\right)\cap C\left(\overline{\Omega^{\sigma}}\right)
. For the same reason also the function
v
defined in (
5.29
) belongs to
C^{3}(\Omega)\cap C(\overline{\Omega})
. We denote again by
\omega
the modulus of continuity of
v_{\sigma}
and by
\omega_{2}
the one of
v
. Moreover, the elliptic equation satisfies the maximum principle, hence for all
x\in\Omega
we can estimate

\left|v(x)-v_{\sigma}(x)\right|\leq\max\limits_{x\in\partial\Omega^{\sigma}}\left|v_{\sigma}\left(\pi_{\partial\Omega}(x)\right)-v_{\sigma}(x)\right|\leq\omega(\sigma).

We can now prove a suitable new version of Lemma
4.6
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
x\in\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
,
0<\delta<\frac{1}{12}
and
\beta=\frac{1-6\delta}{3}>0
. Then

\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(v_{\sigma}-u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\right|\leq C(\Omega,g_{\nu},c_{0},c_{1})e^{-\frac{c_{0}d(x)}{2\varepsilon}}\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}+C_{\sigma}\varepsilon\right)+C(\Omega,\sigma,c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{3},

for some constants
C(\Omega,g_{\nu},c_{0},c_{1})>0
and
C(\Omega,\sigma,c_{0},c_{1})
and
0<\varepsilon<1
sufficiently small.

Proof.

Since
x\in\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
then
d(x)>\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}
. Hence, as we have seen at the beginning of Section 5.3 we obtain for
\beta
as in the Lemma

\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\right|\leq\Arrowvert g\Arrowvert_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{0}d}{\varepsilon}}\leq C(g_{\nu},c_{0})e^{-\frac{c_{0}d}{2\varepsilon}}\varepsilon^{\beta}.

We consider now the operator acting on
v_{\sigma}
. Since
\Omega\subsetneq\Omega^{\sigma}
there exists a constant
c_{\sigma}>0
depending on
v_{\sigma}
such that
\sup\limits_{0\leq n\leq 3}\sup\limits_{x\in\Omega}\Arrowvert\nabla_{x}^{n}v_{\sigma}\Arrowvert_{\infty}\leq c_{\sigma}
. We expand
v_{\sigma}
in
\Omega
with Taylor as
v_{\sigma}(\eta)=v_{\sigma}(x)+\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\cdot(\eta-x)+\frac{1}{2}(\eta-x)^{\top}\nabla_{x}^{2}v_{\sigma}(x)(\eta-x)+E^{3}(\eta,x)
, where
\left|E^{3}(\eta,x)\right|\leq c_{\sigma}|x-\eta|^{3}
. We want to use the expansion of
v_{\sigma}
together with the fact that this function solves the elliptic equation as given in (
5.30
). In the case of constant coefficient, the estimate on the operator was the result of the symmetry of the kernel. Indeed, the integral in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of the term with the first derivative in the Taylor expansion was zero and for the term with the second derivative we obtained the Laplacian, which was in that case also zero. As we have already noticed, when the absorption coefficient
\alpha
is space dependent the kernel
K_{\varepsilon}
is no longer symmetric and moreover
v_{\sigma}
solves a more general elliptic equation.

Our strategy now is to find a decomposition of the kernel
K_{\varepsilon}
in such a way that we can recover the elliptic equation (
5.30
) and with a remainder which gives errors of the order
\varepsilon^{3}
. This decomposition is given by claim (iii) in Proposition
5.1
. We recall
K_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)=K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}^{2}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+\tilde{R}^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)
. Analogously as we have computed in (
5.14
) we see in this case

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\tilde{R}^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)|x-\eta|^{n}\;d\eta\leq C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{n+2}.

(5.31)

With this decomposition we recover the elliptic equation. Indeed, using equation (
4.71
) we compute

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\left(K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}^{2}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)\right)\frac{1}{2}(\eta-x)^{\top}\nabla_{x}^{2}v_{\sigma}(x)(\eta-x)=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3\alpha^{2}(x)}\Delta v_{\sigma}(x),

(5.32)

where we used that
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
are antisymmetric while
(\eta-x)^{\top}\nabla_{x}^{2}v_{\sigma}(x)(\eta-x)
is symmetric.

Before moving to the term containing
\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\cdot(\eta-x)
we notice that for any symmetric function
F(x-\eta)
we can compute

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;&F(x-\eta)\left(\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot(\eta-x)\right)\left(\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\cdot(\eta-x)\right)\\
=&\sum_{i\neq j}\partial_{i}\alpha(x)\partial_{j}v_{\sigma}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;F(x-\eta)(\eta-x)_{i}(\eta-x)_{j}\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{i}\alpha(x)\partial_{i}v_{\sigma}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;F(x-\eta)(\eta-x)_{i}^{2}\\
=&\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;F(x-\eta)|\eta-x|^{2},\end{split}

(5.33)

where we used the symmetry of
F
. Hence, since
K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}
is symmetric using the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
and equation (
5.33
) we conclude

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;&\left(K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}^{2}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)\right)\left(\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\cdot(\eta-x)\right)\\
=&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;\frac{\alpha(x)e^{-\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha(x)}-\frac{|x-\eta|}{2\varepsilon}\right)\left(\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot(\eta-x)\right)\left(\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\cdot(\eta-x)\right)\\
=&\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;\frac{\alpha(x)e^{-\frac{\alpha(x)|x-\eta|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon|x-\eta|^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha(x)}-\frac{|x-\eta|}{2\varepsilon}\right)|\eta-x|^{2}\\
=&\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3\alpha^{3}(x)}\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;\frac{e^{-|\eta|}}{4\pi|\eta|^{2}}\left(1-\frac{|\eta|}{2}\right)|\eta|^{2}=-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3\alpha^{3}(x)}\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x).\end{split}

(5.34)

Hence, equations (
5.32
) and (
5.34
) imply

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;&\left(K^{\alpha(x)}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(x-\eta)+\mathcal{K}^{2}_{\varepsilon}(x-\eta)\right)\left(\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\cdot(\eta-x)+\frac{1}{2}(\eta-x)^{\top}\nabla_{x}^{2}v_{\sigma}(x)(\eta-x)\right)\\
=&\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3\alpha(x)}\left(\frac{\Delta v_{\sigma}(x)}{\alpha(x)}-\frac{\nabla_{x}\alpha(x)}{\alpha(^{2}x)}\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\right)=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3\alpha(x)}\Div\left(\frac{1}{\alpha(x)}\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\right)=0.\end{split}

(5.35)

We are ready now to conclude the estimate of the operator acting on
v_{\sigma}
. Using indeed that
|x-\eta|>d(x)
for
\eta\in\Omega^{c}
we compute

\begin{split}\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\right.&\left.\left(v_{\sigma}\right)(x)\right|\leq\left|v_{\sigma}(x)-\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)\left[v_{\sigma}(x)+\nabla_{x}v_{\sigma}(x)\cdot(\eta-x)+\frac{1}{2}(\eta-x)^{\top}\nabla_{x}^{2}v_{\sigma}(x)(\eta-x)\right]\;d\eta\right|\\
&+\left|\int_{\Omega}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)E^{3}(\eta,x)\;d\eta\right|\\
\leq&v_{\sigma}(x)\int_{B^{c}_{d(x)}(x)}K_{\varepsilon}\left(\eta-x\right)\;d\eta+C(c_{\sigma})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\eta\;\left|\tilde{R}^{x}_{\varepsilon}(x;\eta)\right|\left(|x-\eta|+|x-\eta|^{2}\right)\\
&+C(c_{0},c_{1},\sigma)(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}+\varepsilon^{3})\int_{B^{c}_{d_{\varepsilon}(x)}(0)}\frac{e^{-|y|}}{4\pi|y|^{2}}\left(|y|+|y|^{2}+|y|^{3}+|y|^{4}\right)\;dy\\
&+C(c_{0},c_{1},\sigma)\varepsilon^{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}}{4\pi\varepsilon^{3}}\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}\;dy\leq C(c_{0},c_{1},\sigma,\Omega)e^{-\frac{c_{0}d(x)}{2\varepsilon}}\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}+\varepsilon\right)+C(c_{0},c_{1},\sigma)\varepsilon^{3},\end{split}

where we first used
d(x)>\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}
, then we decomposed the kernel according to claim (iii) in Proposition
5.1
, we applied the result in (
5.35
), the estimate for the remainder
\tilde{R}^{x}_{\varepsilon}
as given in (
5.31
) and finally the estimate
K_{\varepsilon}\leq C(c_{0},c_{1})K^{c_{0}}_{\varepsilon}
. This ended the proof of Lemma
5.4
.
∎

Similarly as in Lemma
4.84
, Lemma
5.3
, the maximum principle for elliptic operators and the uniform continuity of
v
imply the following result

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon>0
small enough. Then the following uniform bound holds

\left|v_{\sigma}(x)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq\omega(\sigma)+\omega_{2}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}\right)+C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}-4\delta}.

We have now all elements for completing the proof of the convergence of
u^{\varepsilon}
to
v
.

Theorem 5.2
.

u^{\varepsilon}
converges to
v
uniformly in every compact set.

Proof.

We argue exactly as in Theorem
4.3
applying the maximum principle to the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}
. To this end we see first of all that

\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\varepsilon}}d\eta\;K_{\varepsilon}(x\;\eta)\left|v_{\sigma}(\eta)-u^{\varepsilon}(\eta)\right|\leq\frac{C(c_{0},c_{1},g)}{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{6\delta-1}{3}}}{2}\right)\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\varepsilon}}d\eta\;\frac{1}{|x-\eta|^{2}}\leq C(c_{0},c_{1},\Omega,g)\varepsilon^{3},

where we used
|x-\eta|>\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}}{2}
for
\eta\in\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon
sufficiently small and the well-known estimate
|x|^{n}e^{-|x|}\leq C_{n}
with
n=13
and
\delta<\frac{1}{78}
. Hence, Lemma
5.4
implies for
x\in\hat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
and
\beta=\frac{1-6\delta}{3}

\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}\left(v_{\sigma}-u^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\right|\leq C(\Omega,g_{\nu},c_{0},c_{1})e^{-\frac{c_{0}d(x)}{2\varepsilon}}\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}+C_{\sigma}\varepsilon\right)+C(\Omega,\sigma,c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{3}.

Moreover, Lemma
5.5
assures that

\left|v_{\sigma}(x)-u^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq\omega(\sigma)+\omega_{2}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}+2\delta}\right)+C\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+C(c_{0},c_{1})\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}-4\delta}

for
x\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
.

As we have seen in (
5.19
) the supersolution
\Phi^{\varepsilon}(x)
satisfies also
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon}\left(\Phi^{\varepsilon}\right)(x)\geq C\varepsilon^{2}
. Hence, we refer now to the proof Theorem
4.3
, which works here in the same way just replacing the supersolution with the suitable
\Phi^{\varepsilon}
defined in Theorem
5.1
. Since the arguments are the same we omit the details.
∎
